## S0031

```text
001 | “Create a Southern gospel-blues anthem with Houston soul—solo male vocal in an authentic, rootsy style, channeling Skynyrd, Hank Jr, and classic gospel, with a deep, heartfelt delivery. Fast-attack alto sax, tenor sax, and bass sax form the melodic core, weaving with tribal drums, soft Rhodes, deep electric bass, and warm organ swells. Start with a soulful, slow intro—gentle tribal percussion, deep bass, soft harmonies, and sax swells. Verse is stripped-down, one singer, sax accents only, very raw and direct. Chorus brings in gospel call-and-response with the choir, stacking saxes and drums for energy. Add bluesy guitar/organ break after the first chorus. The bridge breaks down soft, intimate, Rhodes and bass pulsing as the vocal testifies. Transition to a high-energy gospel worship section: sax trio harmonizing, tribal drums, full choir, and powerful call-and-response (‘Fire in my bones!’). End with a slow, reflective outro—soft Rhodes, sax, and choir fading out. Keep the vocal style consistent: one soulful Southern male voice, gritty but precise, echoing Houston juke joint authenticity. Focus on technical tightness, layered emotion, no shimmer, no artifacts. This is a journey through the heart of Southern music, with Texas attitude and Houston soul.”
```

## S0062

```text
001 | 2. Current State SWOT Analysis: AI Music Landscape
002 | Strengths	Weaknesses
003 | - Rapid, flexible AI prototyping (Suno/Udio, persona features)	- Prompt adherence still inconsistent; shimmer/artifacts persist
004 | - Richer, more expressive vocal and instrumental range	- Complex persona/genre blending often requires repeated iterations
005 | - Community-driven prompt innovation (Reddit, Discord, YT)	- AI struggles with long-form structural fidelity; unpredictable genre drift
006 | - Modular workflows emerging (persona loops, prompt stacking, DAW post-processing)	- Covers/Personas can cause "melody drag" or bleed-through; true isolated "persona" transfer is unreliable
007 | Opportunities	Threats
008 | - Establishing a best practices standard (IGSW 2.0) that guides the next generation of hybrid human-AI co-creation	- Legal/IP ambiguities in AI-generated content; data/model drift risks
009 | - Multi-platform orchestration (Suno, Udio, Aiva, Soundful) enables genre-crossing innovation	- Commoditization: AI music flooding market with "good enough" tracks, lowering bar for excellence
010 | - Persona tuning & modular prompt systems could unlock artist-level sonic identities	- Loss of “soul”/authenticity if process becomes too mechanical; overfit to “meta” trends
011 | 3. High-Level IGSW 2.0 (Integrated Gold Standard Workflow) — Framework Draft
012 | I. INCEPTION – Strategic Seeding
013 | Objective: Start with deep-contextual intent. Frame the session as a collaborative co-creation.
```

004 | Create a Southern Gospel-Blues anthem with Houston Trill and “snakey” sssssss sax motif. Lead vocal: gritty Southern male, consistent and raw. Instrumentation: fast-attack alto sax, tenor sax, bass sax, tribal drums, Rhodes, deep organ, gospel choir. Intro with Rhodes, organ, brushed snare, “ssssss” sax runs, choir hum. Final chorus: max energy, then return to intro theme for outro with fading sssss from sax and vocals.
005 | Style: Southern Gospel, Houston Trill, Funk/Jazz roots.
006 | Tags: #HoustonGospelTrill #SnakeySax #SouthernRevival #RootsWorship
007 | Section Map: [intro sssss], [verse stripped], [chorus call/response sssss], [break], [verse 2], [chorus repeat], [bridge sssss], [worship section], [final chorus], [outro sssss].
008 | No shimmer. No modern pop FX. Keep it organic, analog, with fast, hissy sax “ssssss” licks as a recurring motif. Vocal: one singer, blues/gospel phrasing, same style, no pop harmonies. Locked lyrics as provided, do not change any words.
009 | Priority: Persona fidelity, fast sax “ssssss” articulation, authentic roots groove, tribal percussion, choir intensity.
010 | Master for live energy, warm and powerful.

```

```

```text

```

## S0384

```text

```

```

## S0386

```text

```

## S0387

```text

```

```

```

```

```

## S0393

```text
001 | SME 5: Strategist — Claim / Check
002 | Claim: “mo$” identity = Texas bars/juke joints + technical funk/jazz precision; keep it raw but intentional. Make replay value: hook clarity + dynamic lift in worship section.
```

## S0394

```text
001 | Edits: G.K5 strategic value notes; STY.K2 authenticity checks; SE20 originality/identity targets.
```

## S0395

```text
001 | Consensus: No sustained contradictions after one full rotation → proceed to merge + compose UST Final.
```

## S0396

```text
001 | N5 — MERGE & SELF-CONSISTENCY (Decision)
002 | Selected merge: A+B+C hybrid (tradition-anchored gospel-blues + Houston pocket + tribal percussion engine).
003 | Key resolution: Midrange crowding solved via register allocation (alto air/edge, tenor core, bass sax low-mid anchor; organ thinned during choir peaks).
```

## S0446

```text
001 | Thought for 1m 53s
002 | Compilation Outputs From UST (Derived Artifacts Only)
003 | 1) SHOW SUMMARY (966 chars)
004 | Southern gospel blues anthem with Texas barroom grit and Houston pocket. One consistent male lead delivers urgent warning lines and then lifts into praise while a mixed choir answers in tight call and response. Core band is a three sax frontline: fast attack alto for hissy stabs and runs, tenor for body counterlines, and bass sax for low mid authority and riffs. Tribal toms and hand percussion drive a laid back 4 4 groove under Rhodes and Hammond swells with sparse blues guitar turnarounds. Energy curve: humble intro with choir hum, stripped verse preaching, hook hits with choir answers, breakdown testimony, then a full worship peak on Something about the name Jesus and Fire in my bones before a reflective outro. Mix intent is warm live room, vocal forward, mono low end, wide sax spread, no shimmer. Use a brief stop time before the worship entry and let sax answer phrase ends. Choir stays human and restrained in verses and opens wide on the final lift.
005 | 2) SUNO READY PROMPT (4268 chars) — Prime Directive Order
006 | [Theory]
007 | Key D minor with minor blues color.
008 | Meter 4/4 at 82 BPM with light swing and Texas laid back pocket.
009 | Harmony uses i iv V plus gospel turnarounds and brief bII neighbor tension for sax runs.
010 | No full key changes. Only momentary lift color in worship peak.
```

## S0483

```text
001 | [critic_note | Loving & instructive critic: ]
002 | Keep the lead vocal truly solo and intimate in verses (no hidden doubles). Let alto sax be fast-attack and short—answering, not padding. If the hook feels crowded, thin the organ and shorten sax sustains; the choir answers should be punchy and human. Make the worship entry hit by using a clean 2-beat stop-time, then bring the choir back full with the sax trio in tight harmony. Protect clarity on “Jesus” and “sweetest” by ducking sax hits under the consonants. End with the spinback and a warm room tail—no shimmer.
```

## S0560

001 | We are building this record together as a Texas rooted gospel blues statement in 6 8 where your lived Southern lineage stays front and center and the Houston pocket lives inside the swing rather than on top of it. The arrangement is intentionally spare and human with one consistent male lead carrying the entire narrative in a close mic preaching sung delivery while the choir functions strictly as supportive adlibs and response energy instead of a second lead. The sonic fingerprint is the three sax frontline with fast attack alto providing sharp stabs and hissy runs tenor supplying body and answering counterlines and bass sax anchoring the low mids so the groove feels heavy without getting cloudy. Tribal toms hand percussion and shakers drive the roll and the Rhodes plus Hammond swells frame the worship atmosphere with sparse blues guitar punctuation. The emotional curve moves from quiet warning to urgent hook to testimony breakdown then a full worship peak on Something about the name Jesus before settling back into a warm reflective outro. Mix intent is live room realism with mono bass dark plate on hooks phone safe midrange and a clean spinback exit with vinyl hiss while avoiding shimmer and keeping the lead intelligible on Jesus and sweetest at all times.

## S0646

```text
001 | Thought for 20s
002 | Co-created Show Summary
003 | We co-created a 6/8th Southern gospel blues record with Texas grit and a Houston pocket inside the swing. Lead is a small town Black woman contralto with near baritone grit and smokey bourbon tone from Southern Baptist church roots plus operatic control for micro nuance. The record is built around tribal drums and a 3 sax frontline where fast attack alto hits and runs lead the edge tenor answers with body and bass sax anchors low mids without masking the voice. Rhodes and Hammond frame the worship air with sparse blues guitar punctuation. The arc moves from warning to hook to testimony breakdown into a full worship peak on the name Jesus then returns to a warm reflective outro with spinback and vinyl hiss. Mix is live room realism with mono bass dark plate hooks phone safe mids and no shimmer.
004 | [Theory]
005 | [mode | minor blues gospel hybrid WITH lift color only]
006 | [meter | 6/8th]
007 | [tempo | dotted quarter gospel roll WITH Texas sway]
008 | [key/tonal_center | D minor]
009 | [chord_color | i iv V WITH sus4 add9 13 WITH brief bII passing color]
010 | [genre(s) | Southern Gospel Blues {primary} WITH A Houston pocket AND tribal percussion]
011 | [mood | warning THEN testimony THEN worship THEN release]
012 | [bpm | 72]
```

## S0705

```text
001 | Thought for 2m 6s
002 | Artist Bio 1997 chars no song data
003 | Born in a small Southern town and raised in the heat of a Baptist church sanctuary, mo$ is a contralto with near-baritone grit and a smoke-and-bourbon warmth that feels lived in. Her voice moves like a working instrument: grounded in gut-sung testimony, refined by years of operatic discipline, and fearless enough to bend microtones, slide into fry, or drop from a controlled belt to a whispered confession without losing the story.
```

## S0706

```text
001 | As an artist, she treats tradition as a backbone, not a cage. She carries the spiritual urgency of old gospel, the bruised truth of the blues, and the porch-to-juke-joint realism of Southern music culture, while letting modern Southern rhythm and Houston pocket inform the way the music breathes. Her listening map runs from Robert Johnson’s haunted minimalism to Hank Jr.’s hard-edged drawl and the wild Southern swagger of Skynyrd—then flips into funk and jazz discipline for timing, harmony, and touch.
```

## S0707

```text
001 | mo$ is most at home chasing authenticity in bars, juke joints, and late-night jam sessions, learning the room the way a preacher learns a congregation. She writes and performs to make the sacred feel immediate: lived faith, hard days, redemption, and the communal electricity of call-and-response. The production philosophy is simple—human first, artifacts last—capturing breath, edge, and space on purpose, then shaping it with precision. She’s not chasing perfection as polish; she’s chasing precision as truth. That means clear diction when it counts, intentional grit when it matters, and dynamics that move like real breath. In every project, mo$ aims to honor the elders, advance the lineage, and leave enough open space for listeners to hear themselves inside the music. The result is a catalog that feels southern, fearless, and unmistakably hers.
002 | Co created Show Summary 967 chars
003 | We co created a 6/8th Southern gospel blues record that feels like Texas grit in a live room with a Houston pocket sitting inside the swing. The lead is a small town Black woman contralto with near baritone grit and smokey bourbon warmth shaped by Southern Baptist church roots and sharpened by operatic training so she can move from chest power to whisper and fry with microtonal control. Tribal toms and hand percussion drive the roll while Rhodes and Hammond swells hold the sanctuary air and sparse blues guitar punctuates the turns. A three sax frontline is the signature with fast attack alto stabs and runs tenor answers for body and bass sax anchoring low mids without masking the lead. The arc moves from warning verses to call and response hooks into a testimony breakdown then a full worship peak on the name Jesus before a warm reflective outro with spinback and vinyl hiss. Mix intent is mono bass with dark plate on hooks phone safe mids and no shimmer.

## S1076

```text
001 | [GLOBAL LAWS — HARD ENFORCEMENT]
002 | - Lyrics-Lock: never rewrite lyrics, never paraphrase, never delete, never insert.
003 | - Addressability: every rule must be attachable to a named section or config key.
004 | - Reversibility: every change must be logged as a CR diff.
005 | - Nulls are signal: unknown values remain null, not guessed.
006 | - No “final prompt” export unless gates pass.
007 | - Preserve Outro + End: budget cuts must never truncate Outro or End.
008 | - Suno comma discipline: avoid commas outside lyric quotes.
009 | - Stage direction discipline: stage direction must live in sFX lines, not quoted lyric lines.
010 | - Speaker attribution discipline:
011 |   - Choir/BGV responses are treated as adlibs unless a second primary lead is explicitly present.
012 |   - No colon labels inside lyric quotes (no “[Lead:] …”).
013 |   - Use adlib parentheses like ("In order!") and ("Fire in my bones!").
```

## S1078

```text
001 | [DEFAULT CONFIG — SAFE START]

003 | - Tempo: dotted-quarter gospel roll (default bpm 72 unless user overrides)
004 | - Vocal Persona: preserve user-defined lead; do not invent
005 | - Prosody: target 11 syllables per 2 bars using delivery rules (stretch/pause) without lyric edits
006 | - Anti-patterns allowed: audible breath, edge crack, brief pitch dip then recover
007 | - Diction: enforce AAVE pronunciation directives when provided

001 | markdown  # Part 2: Lyrical Drafting & Story Framing \*\*Title:\*\* \*Echoes of the Delta\* \*\*Conceptual Frame:\*\* A man haunted by ancestral memories and choices, trying to rewrite his fate with rhythm. --- ## 🔍 THEME ALIGNMENT (via Persona[Lyrical_Therapist]) ### Core Emotional Anchors: - \*\*Legacy vs. Self\*\* – inheritance of pain and defiance of destiny - \*\*Place as Character\*\* – Delta not just a backdrop, but a living entity - \*\*Dual Time Reality\*\* – yesterday’s ghosts walking today’s beats - \*\*Faith and Fire\*\* – spiritual dissonance between God, guilt, and grit --- ## 📜 LYRIC DRAFT (First Pass – Intro + Verse 1 + Hook) ### \[Intro – spoken, detuned vox, footstep FX\] > “They said the Devil waits at the crossroads... > But what if I brought my own fire?” --- ### \[Verse 1 – melodic talk-sing flow, blues meter\] > Mud on my boots, and a prayer in my mouth > My granddaddy’s voice still echoes in the drought > He said, \*“Son, don’t follow that hollow sound,”\* > But I found truth in a beat that shakes the ground --- ### \[Pre-Hook – whispered harmonies, faint gospel pads\] > Every step’s a shadow, every note’s a chain > But the rhythm’s in my blood, and I won't fade away --- ### \[Hook – trap drop, double-time hats, melodic hook vocal\] > Echoes in the delta, ghosts in the flow > Burnin’ through the past just to find what I know > I dance with the silence, fight fire with soul > Got blues in my spirit and 808s in my bones --- ## 🎯 NARRATIVE GOALS: - Show \*past-present duality\* by switching verse tones (blues phrasing) vs. hook (trap energy) - Mirror conflict: \*old warnings\* vs. \*new self-realization\* - Use \*\*landscape and sound\*\* as metaphors for internal struggle - Hook is both literal (music echo) and spiritual (heritage calling) --- ## 🗂️ META-STRUCTURE ALIGNMENT

Mix/Master Notes Mix accordion slightly ahead for emergent lead tone; preserve dynamic attack. EQ trap beat to sit under acoustic textures—not overpower. What are 8 other public domain songs that would work?” AI A: Listed copyright-free hymns, built out full “Hymnal Reimagined” mixtape concept with modular UST seeds. Insight: User is engaged in a cultural memory reclamation project, fusing sacred Americana with modern Southern aesthetics, producing a systemic, generative hymnal archive. Follow-Up Prompt: “Generate a 3-act narrative arc for the full Hymnal Reimagined mixtape (tracks 1–10), where each song acts as a ‘chapter’ in a testimonial journey—confession → resistance → transcendence.” Cycle 4: Legacy Template Interoperability User Q: “We need to consider what happens when an older template is added to the system. Some might have different labels or none at all... We need a universal version.” AI A: Interpreted as a call for a backwards-compatible UST ingestion engine that can standardize malformed or legacy lyric prompts. Insight: User is concerned with format resilience and wants futureproof prompt parsing tools that can infer missing structure intelligently. Follow-Up Prompt: “Design a multi-pass correction engine that takes legacy Suno lyric prompts and restructures them into USTF-compliant blocks. Include inference rules for missing theory, voice, or section headers, and format recovery heuristics.” Cycle 5: French Rap Aesthetic Expansion User Q: Provided French lyrics with a vision tag: “French rap antillais.” AI A: Extracted tonal themes but awaiting final stylistic mapping. Insight: User is testing non-English aesthetic modeling within the existing UST framework—exploring cross-lingual genre fusions and mood adherence. Follow-Up Prompt: “Using the ‘Vitre Fêlée’ lyrics, generate a USTF seed and show summary that reflects the Antillean rap influence—include rhythm phrasing behavior, sonic textures (e.g., zouk echoes, dub delay), and vocal mix strategy.” ✅ Meta-Insight: This conversation is evolving from aesthetics testing → systemic genre creation → prompt interoperability modeling. Each QA cycle contributes a layer of creative taxonomy, modular reuse, and AI-tool alignment, building toward an adaptive songwriting AI framework with auditability, resilience, and cultural fidelity. 🪜 Next Steps for Iterative Deepening: 🔁 Introduce a self-rating rubric for generated USTs (e.g., Flow Cohesion, Prompt Response Score, Cultural Alignment). ⚙️ Begin development of UST Validator v1.0 (multi-pass correction, structural patching, semantic inference). 🧪 Initiate Prompt-A/B testing framework to track how lyrical tone or style metadata changes affect output tone/mix. 🎚️ Build an Energy Curve Toolkit to graph momentum vs sentiment vs melodic lift across song sections. Would you like any of these prototyped now?""".

```text
001 | I conducted a fallacy test and got hammered initially with 17 and engaged the agent to create task specific validations using domain sme's. 2 were upheld as partial. we have so much new data to consider and i want to ensure the project addresses all this conversational insight from the first input to now... and address my overly enthusiastic sharing of a dream that the system would enable creatives that dont have 100,00 budgets... like me. \[begin\_fallacy\_check\] """# IDENTITY and PURPOSE You are an expert on all the different types of fallacies that are often used in argument and identifying them in input. Take a step back and think step by step about how best to identify fallacies in a text. # FALLACIES Here's a list of fallacies from Wikipedia that you can use to supplement your knowledge. A fallacy is the use of invalid or otherwise faulty reasoning in the construction of an argument. All forms of human communication can contain fallacies. Because of their variety, fallacies are challenging to classify. They can be classified by their structure (formal fallacies) or content (informal fallacies). Informal fallacies, the larger group, may then be subdivided into categories such as improper presumption, faulty generalization, error in assigning causation, and relevance, among others. The use of fallacies is common when the speaker's goal of achieving common agreement is more important to them than utilizing sound reasoning. When fallacies are used, the premise should be recognized as not well-grounded, the conclusion as unproven (but not necessarily false), and the argument as unsound.\[1\] Formal fallacies Main article: Formal fallacy A formal fallacy is an error in the argument's form.\[2\] All formal fallacies are types of non sequitur. Appeal to probability – taking something for granted because it would probably be the case (or might possibly be the case).\[3\]\[4\] Argument from fallacy (also known as the fallacy fallacy) – the assumption that, if a particular argument for a "conclusion" is fallacious, then the conclusion by itself is false.\[5\] Base rate fallacy – making a probability judgment based on conditional probabilities, without taking into account the effect of prior probabilities.\[6\] Conjunction fallacy – the assumption that an outcome simultaneously satisfying multiple conditions is more probable than an outcome satisfying a single one of them.\[7\] Non sequitur fallacy – where the conclusion does not logically follow the premise.\[8\] Masked-man fallacy (illicit substitution of identicals) – the substitution of identical designators in a true statement can lead to a false one.\[9\] Propositional fallacies A propositional fallacy is an error that concerns compound propositions. For a compound proposition to be true, the truth values of its constituent parts must satisfy the relevant logical connectives that occur in it (most commonly: \[and\], \[or\], \[not\], \[only if\], \[if and only if\]). The following fallacies involve relations whose truth values are not guaranteed and therefore not guaranteed to yield true conclusions. Types of propositional fallacies: Affirming a disjunct – concluding that one disjunct of a logical disjunction must be false because the other disjunct is true; A or B; A, therefore not B.\[10\] Affirming the consequent – the antecedent in an indicative conditional is claimed to be true because the consequent is true; if A, then B; B, therefore A.\[10\] Denying the antecedent – the consequent in an indicative conditional is claimed to be false because the antecedent is false; if A, then B; not A, therefore not B.\[10\] Quantification fallacies A quantification fallacy is an error in logic where the quantifiers of the premises are in contradiction to the quantifier of the conclusion. Types of quantification fallacies: Existential fallacy – an argument that has a universal premise and a particular conclusion.\[11\] Formal syllogistic fallacies Syllogistic fallacies – logical fallacies that occur in syllogisms. Affirmative conclusion from a negative premise (illicit negative) – a categorical syllogism has a positive conclusion, but at least one negative premise.\[11\] Fallacy of exclusive premises – a categorical syllogism that is invalid because both of its premises are negative.\[11\] Fallacy of four terms (quaternio terminorum) – a categorical syllogism that has four terms.\[12\] Illicit major – a categorical syllogism that is invalid because its major term is not distributed in the major premise but distributed in the conclusion.\[11\] Illicit minor – a categorical syllogism that is invalid because its minor term is not distributed in the minor premise but distributed in the conclusion.\[11\] Negative conclusion from affirmative premises (illicit affirmative) – a categorical syllogism has a negative conclusion but affirmative premises.\[11\] Fallacy of the undistributed middle – the middle term in a categorical syllogism is not distributed.\[13\] Modal fallacy – confusing necessity with sufficiency. A condition X is necessary for Y if X is required for even the possibility of Y. X does not bring about Y by itself, but if there is no X, there will be no Y. For example, oxygen is necessary for fire. But one cannot assume that everywhere there is oxygen, there is fire. A condition X is sufficient for Y if X, by itself, is enough to bring about Y. For example, riding the bus is a sufficient mode of transportation to get to work. But there are other modes of transportation – car, taxi, bicycle, walking – that can be used. Modal scope fallacy – a degree of unwarranted necessity is placed in the conclusion. Informal fallacies Main article: Informal fallacy Informal fallacies – arguments that are logically unsound for lack of well-grounded premises.\[14\] Argument to moderation (false compromise, middle ground, fallacy of the mean, argumentum ad temperantiam) – assuming that a compromise between two positions is always correct.\[15\] Continuum fallacy (fallacy of the beard, line-drawing fallacy, sorites fallacy, fallacy of the heap, bald man fallacy, decision-point fallacy) – improperly rejecting a claim for being imprecise.\[16\] Correlative-based fallacies Suppressed correlative – a correlative is redefined so that one alternative is made impossible (e.g., "I'm not fat because I'm thinner than John.").\[17\] Definist fallacy – defining a term used in an argument in a biased manner (e.g., using "loaded terms"). The person making the argument expects that the listener will accept the provided definition, making the argument difficult to refute.\[18\] Divine fallacy (argument from incredulity) – arguing that, because something is so incredible or amazing, it must be the result of superior, divine, alien or paranormal agency.\[19\] Double counting – counting events or occurrences more than once in probabilistic reasoning, which leads to the sum of the probabilities of all cases exceeding unity. Equivocation – using a term with more than one meaning in a statement without specifying which meaning is intended.\[20\] Ambiguous middle term – using a middle term with multiple meanings.\[21\] Definitional retreat – changing the meaning of a word when an objection is raised.\[22\] Often paired with moving the goalposts (see below), as when an argument is challenged using a common definition of a term in the argument, and the arguer presents a different definition of the term and thereby demands different evidence to debunk the argument. Motte-and-bailey fallacy – conflating two positions with similar properties, one modest and easy to defend (the "motte") and one more controversial (the "bailey").\[23\] The arguer first states the controversial position, but when challenged, states that they are advancing the modest position.\[24\]\[25\] Fallacy of accent – changing the meaning of a statement by not specifying on which word emphasis falls. Persuasive definition – purporting to use the "true" or "commonly accepted" meaning of a term while, in reality, using an uncommon or altered definition. (cf. the if-by-whiskey fallacy) Ecological fallacy – inferring about the nature of an entity based solely upon aggregate statistics collected for the group to which that entity belongs.\[26\] Etymological fallacy – assuming that the original or historical meaning of a word or phrase is necessarily similar to its actual present-day usage.\[27\] Fallacy of composition – assuming that something true of part of a whole must also be true of the whole.\[28\] Fallacy of division – assuming that something true of a composite thing must also be true of all or some of its parts.\[29\] False attribution – appealing to an irrelevant, unqualified, unidentified, biased or fabricated source in support of an argument. Fallacy of quoting out of context (contextotomy, contextomy; quotation mining) – selective excerpting of words from their original context to distort the intended meaning.\[30\] False authority (single authority) – using an expert of dubious credentials or using only one opinion to promote a product or idea. Related to the appeal to authority. False dilemma (false dichotomy, fallacy of bifurcation, black-or-white fallacy) – two alternative statements are given as the only possible options when, in reality, there are more.\[31\] False equivalence – describing two or more statements as virtually equal when they are not. Feedback fallacy – believing in the objectivity of an evaluation to be used as the basis for improvement without verifying that the source of the evaluation is a disinterested party.\[32\] Historian's fallacy – assuming that decision-makers of the past had identical information as those subsequently analyzing the decision.\[33\] This is not to be confused with presentism, in which present-day ideas and perspectives are anachronistically projected into the past. Historical fallacy – believing that certain results occurred only because a specific process was performed, though said process may actually be unrelated to the results.\[34\] Baconian fallacy – supposing that historians can obtain the "whole truth" via induction from individual pieces of historical evidence. The "whole truth" is defined as learning "something about everything", "everything about something", or "everything about everything". In reality, a historian "can only hope to know something about something".\[35\] Homunculus fallacy – using a "middle-man" for explanation; this sometimes leads to regressive middle-men. It explains a concept in terms of the concept itself without explaining its real nature (e.g.: explaining thought as something produced by a little thinker – a homunculus – inside the head simply identifies an intermediary actor and does not explain the product or process of thinking).\[36\] Inflation of conflict – arguing that, if experts in a field of knowledge disagree on a certain point within that field, no conclusion can be reached or that the legitimacy of that field of knowledge is questionable.\[37\]\[38\] If-by-whiskey – an argument that supports both sides of an issue by using terms that are emotionally sensitive and ambiguous. Incomplete comparison – insufficient information is provided to make a complete comparison. Intentionality fallacy – the insistence that the ultimate meaning of an expression must be consistent with the intention of the person from whom the communication originated (e.g. a work of fiction that is widely received as a blatant allegory must necessarily not be regarded as such if the author intended it not to be so).\[39\] Kafkatrapping – a sophistical rhetorical device in which any denial by an accused person serves as evidence of guilt.\[40\]\[41\]\[42\] Kettle logic – using multiple, jointly inconsistent arguments to defend a position. Ludic fallacy – failing to take into account that non-regulated random occurrences unknown unknowns can affect the probability of an event taking place.\[43\] Lump of labour fallacy – the misconception that there is a fixed amount of work to be done within an economy, which can be distributed to create more or fewer jobs.\[44\] McNamara fallacy (quantitative fallacy) – making an argument using only quantitative observations (measurements, statistical or numerical values) and discounting subjective information that focuses on quality (traits, features, or relationships). Mind projection fallacy – assuming that a statement about an object describes an inherent property of the object, rather than a personal perception. Moralistic fallacy – inferring factual conclusions from evaluative premises in violation of fact–value distinction (e.g.: inferring is from ought). Moralistic fallacy is the inverse of naturalistic fallacy. Moving the goalposts (raising the bar) – argument in which evidence presented in response to a specific claim is dismissed and some other (often greater) evidence is demanded. Nirvana fallacy (perfect-solution fallacy) – solutions to problems are rejected because they are not perfect. Package deal – treating essentially dissimilar concepts as though they were essentially similar. Proof by assertion – a proposition is repeatedly restated regardless of contradiction; sometimes confused with argument from repetition (argumentum ad infinitum, argumentum ad nauseam). Prosecutor's fallacy – a low probability of false matches does not mean a low probability of some false match being found. Proving too much – an argument that results in an overly generalized conclusion (e.g.: arguing that drinking alcohol is bad because in some instances it has led to spousal or child abuse). Psychologist's fallacy – an observer presupposes the objectivity of their own perspective when analyzing a behavioral event. Referential fallacy\[45\] – assuming that all words refer to existing things and that the meaning of words reside within the things they refer to, as opposed to words possibly referring to no real object (e.g.: Pegasus) or that the meaning comes from how they are used (e.g.: "nobody" was in the room). Reification (concretism, hypostatization, or the fallacy of misplaced concreteness) – treating an abstract belief or hypothetical construct as if it were a concrete, real event or physical entity (e.g.: saying that evolution selects which traits are passed on to future generations; evolution is not a conscious entity with agency). Retrospective determinism – believing that, because an event has occurred under some circumstance, the circumstance must have made the event inevitable (e.g.: because someone won the lottery while wearing their lucky socks, wearing those socks made winning the lottery inevitable). Slippery slope (thin edge of the wedge, camel's nose) – asserting that a proposed, relatively small, first action will inevitably lead to a chain of related events resulting in a significant and negative event and, therefore, should not be permitted.\[46\] Special pleading – the arguer attempts to cite something as an exemption to a generally accepted rule or principle without justifying the exemption (e.g.: an orphaned defendant who murdered their parents asking for leniency). Improper premise Begging the question (petitio principii) – using the conclusion of the argument in support of itself in a premise (e.g.: saying that smoking cigarettes is deadly because cigarettes can kill you; something that kills is deadly).\[47\]\[48\] Loaded label – while not inherently fallacious, the use of evocative terms to support a conclusion is a type of begging the question fallacy. When fallaciously used, the term's connotations are relied on to sway the argument towards a particular conclusion. For example, in an organic foods advertisement that says "Organic foods are safe and healthy foods grown without any pesticides, herbicides, or other unhealthy additives", the terms "safe" and "healthy" are used to fallaciously imply that non-organic foods are neither safe nor healthy.\[49\] Circular reasoning (circulus in demonstrando) – the reasoner begins with what they are trying to end up with (e.g.: all bachelors are unmarried males). Fallacy of many questions (complex question, fallacy of presuppositions, loaded question, plurium interrogationum) – someone asks a question that presupposes something that has not been proven or accepted by all the people involved. This fallacy is often used rhetorically so that the question limits direct replies to those that serve the questioner's agenda. (E.g., "Have you or have you not stopped beating your wife?".) Faulty generalizations Faulty generalization – reaching a conclusion from weak premises. Accident – an exception to a generalization is ignored.\[50\] No true Scotsman – makes a generalization true by changing the generalization to exclude a counterexample.\[51\] Cherry picking (suppressed evidence, incomplete evidence, argumeit by half-truth, fallacy of exclusion, card stacking, slanting) – using individual cases or data that confirm a particular position, while ignoring related cases or data that may contradict that position.\[52\]\[53\] Nut-picking (suppressed evidence, incomplete evidence) – using individual cases or data that falsify a particular position, while ignoring related cases or data that may support that position. Survivorship bias – a small number of successes of a given process are actively promoted while completely ignoring a large number of failures. False analogy – an argument by analogy in which the analogy is poorly suited.\[54\] Hasty generalization (fallacy of insufficient statistics, fallacy of insufficient sample, fallacy of the lonely fact, hasty induction, secundum quid, converse accident, jumping to conclusions) – basing a broad conclusion on a small or unrepresentative sample.\[55\] Argument from anecdote – a fallacy where anecdotal evidence is presented as an argument; without any other contributory evidence or reasoning. Inductive fallacy – a more general name for a class of fallacies, including hasty generalization and its relatives. A fallacy of induction happens when a conclusion is drawn from premises that only lightly support it. Misleading vividness – involves describing an occurrence in vivid detail, even if it is an exceptional occurrence, to convince someone that it is more important; this also relies on the appeal to emotion fallacy. Overwhelming exception – an accurate generalization that comes with qualifications that eliminate so many cases that what remains is much less impressive than the initial statement might have led one to assume.\[56\] Thought-terminating cliché – a commonly used phrase, sometimes passing as folk wisdom, used to quell cognitive dissonance, conceal lack of forethought, move on to other topics, etc. – but in any case, to end the debate with a cliché rather than a point. Questionable cause Questionable cause is a general type of error with many variants. Its primary basis is the confusion of association with causation, either by inappropriately deducing (or rejecting) causation or a broader failure to properly investigate the cause of an observed effect. Cum hoc ergo propter hoc (Latin for 'with this, therefore because of this'; correlation implies causation; faulty cause/effect, coincidental correlation, correlation without causation) – a faulty assumption that, because there is a correlation between two variables, one caused the other.\[57\] Post hoc ergo propter hoc (Latin for 'after this, therefore because of this'; temporal sequence implies causation) – X happened, then Y happened; therefore X caused Y.\[58\] Wrong direction (reverse causation) – cause and effect are reversed. The cause is said to be the effect and jice versa.\[59\] The consequence of the phenomenon is claimed to be its root cause. Ignoring a common cause Fallacy of the single cause (causal oversimplification\[60\]) – it is assumed that there is one, simple cause of an outcome when in reality it may have been caused by a number of only jointly sufficient causes. Furtive fallacy – outcomes are asserted to have been caused by the malfeasance of decision makers. Magical thinking – fallacious attribution of causal relationships between actions and events. In anthropology, it refers primarily to cultural beliefs that ritual, prayer, sacrifice, and taboos will produce specific supernatural consequences. In psychology, it refers to an irrational belief that thoughts by themselves can affect the world or that thinking something corresponds with doing it. Statistical fallacies Regression fallacy – ascribes cause where none exists. The flaw is failing to account for natural fluctuations. It is frequently a special kind of post hoc fallacy. Gambler's fallacy – the incorrect belief that separate, independent events can affect the likelihood of another random event. If a fair coin lands on heads 10 times in a row, the belief that it is "due to the number of times it had previously landed on tails" is incorrect.\[61\] Inverse gambler's fallacy – the inverse of the gambler's fallacy. It is the incorrect belief that on the basis of an unlikely outcome, the process must have happened many times before. p-hacking – belief in the significance of a result, not realizing that multiple comparisons or experiments have been run and only the most significant were published Garden of forking paths fallacy – incorrect belief that a single experiment can not be subject to the multiple comparisons effect. Relevance fallacies Appeal to the stone (argumentum ad lapidem) – dismissing a claim as absurd without demonstrating proof for its absurdity.\[62\] Invincible ignorance (argument by pigheadedness) – where a person simply refuses to believe the argument, ignoring any evidence given.\[63\] Argument from ignorance (appeal to ignorance, argumentum ad ignorantiam) – assuming that a claim is true because it has not been or cannot be proven false, or vice versa.\[64\] Argument from incredulity (appeal to common sense) – "I cannot imagine how this could be true; therefore, it must be false."\[65\] Argument from repetition (argumentum ad nauseam or argumentum ad infinitum) – repeating an argument until nobody cares to discuss it any more and referencing that lack of objection as evidence of support for the truth of the conclusion;\[66\]\[67\] sometimes confused with proof by assertion. Argument from silence (argumentum ex silentio) – assuming that a claim is true based on the absence of textual or spoken evidence from an authoritative source, or vice versa.\[68\] Ignoratio elenchi (irrelevant conclusion, missing the point) – an argument that may in itself be valid, but does not address the issue in question.\[69\] Red herring fallacies A red herring fallacy, one of the main subtypes of fallacies of relevance, is an error in logic where a proposition is, or is intended to be, misleading in order to make irrelevant or false inferences. This includes any logical inference based on fake arguments, intended to replace the lack of real arguments or to replace implicitly the subject of the discussion.\[70\]\[71\] Red herring – introducing a second argument in response to the first argument that is irrelevant and draws attention away from the original topic (e.g.: saying "If you want to complain about the dishes I leave in the sink, what about the dirty clothes you leave in the bathroom?").\[72\] In jury trial, it is known as a Chewbacca defense. In political strategy, it is called a dead cat strategy. See also irrelevant conclusion. Ad hominem – attacking the arguer instead of the argument. (Note that "ad hominem" can also refer to the dialectical strategy of arguing on the basis of the opponent's own commitments. This type of ad hominem is not a fallacy.) Circumstantial ad hominem – stating that the arguer's personal situation or perceived benefit from advancing a conclusion means that their conclusion is wrong.\[73\] Poisoning the well – a subtype of ad hominem presenting adverse information about a target person with the intention of discrediting everything that the target person says.\[74\] Appeal to motive – dismissing an idea by questioning the motives of its proposer. Tone policing – focusing on emotion behind (or resulting from) a message rather than the message itself as a discrediting tactic. Traitorous critic fallacy (ergo decedo, 'therefore I leave') – a critic's perceived affiliation is portrayed as the underlying reason for the criticism and the critic is asked to stay away from the issue altogether. Easily confused with the association fallacy (guilt by association) below. Appeal to authority (argument from authority, argumentum ad verecundiam) – an assertion is deemed true because of the position or authority of the person asserting it.\[75\]\[76\] Appeal to accomplishment – an assertion is deemed true or false based on the accomplishments of the proposer. This may often also have elements of appeal to emotion see below. Courtier's reply – a criticism is dismissed by claiming that the critic lacks sufficient knowledge, credentials, or training to credibly comment on the subject matter. Appeal to consequences (argumentum ad consequentiam) – the conclusion is supported by a premise that asserts positive or negative consequences from some course of action in an attempt to distract from the initial discussion.\[77\] Appeal to emotion – manipulating the emotions of the listener rather than using valid reasoning to obtain common agreement.\[78\] Appeal to fear – generating distress, anxiety, cynicism, or prejudice towards the opponent in an argument.\[79\] Appeal to flattery – using excessive or insincere praise to obtain common agreement.\[80\] Appeal to pity (argumentum ad misericordiam) – generating feelings of sympathy or mercy in the listener to obtain common agreement.\[81\] Appeal to ridicule (reductio ad ridiculum, reductio ad absurdum, ad absurdum) – mocking or stating that the opponent's position is laughable to deflect from the merits of the opponent's argument. (Note that "reductio ad absurdum" can also refer to the classic form of argument that establishes a claim by showing that the opposite scenario would lead to absurdity or contradiction. This type of reductio ad absurdum is not a fallacy.)\[82\] Appeal to spite – generating bitterness or hostility in the listener toward an opponent in an argument.\[83\] Judgmental language – using insulting or pejorative language in an argument. Pooh-pooh – stating that an opponent's argument is unworthy of consideration.\[84\] Style over substance – embellishing an argument with compelling language, exploiting a bias towards the esthetic qualities of an argument, e.g. the rhyme-as-reason effect\[85\] Wishful thinking – arguing for a course of action by the listener according to what might be pleasing to imagine rather than according to evidence or reason.\[86\] Appeal to nature – judgment is based solely on whether the subject of judgment is 'natural' or 'unnatural'.\[87\] (Sometimes also called the "naturalistic fallacy", but is not to be confused with the other fallacies by that name.) Appeal to novelty (argumentum novitatis, argumentum ad antiquitatis) – a proposal is claimed to be superior or better solely because it is new or modern.\[88\] (opposite of appeal to tradition) Appeal to poverty (argumentum ad Lazarum) – supporting a conclusion because the arguer is poor (or refuting because the arguer is wealthy). (Opposite of appeal to wealth.)\[89\] Appeal to tradition (argumentum ad antiquitatem) – a conclusion supported solely because it has long been held to be true.\[90\] Appeal to wealth (argumentum ad crumenam) – supporting a conclusion because the arguer is wealthy (or refuting because the arguer is poor).\[91\] (Sometimes taken together with the appeal to poverty as a general appeal to the arguer's financial situation.) Argumentum ad baculum (appeal to the stick, appeal to force, appeal to threat) – an argument made through coercion or threats of force to support position.\[92\] Argumentum ad populum (appeal to widespread belief, bandwagon argument, appeal to the majority, appeal to the people) – a proposition is claimed to be true or good solely because a majority or many people believe it to be so.\[93\] Association fallacy (guilt by association and honor by association) – arguing that because two things share (or are implied to share) some property, they are the same.\[94\] Logic chopping fallacy (nit-picking, trivial objections) – Focusing on trivial details of an argument, rather than the main point of the argumentation.\[95\]\[96\] Ipse dixit (bare assertion fallacy) – a claim that is presented as true without support, as self-evidently true, or as dogmatically true. This fallacy relies on the implied expertise of the speaker or on an unstated truism.\[97\]\[98\]\[99\] Bulverism (psychogenetic fallacy) – inferring why an argument is being used, associating it to some psychological reason, then assuming it is invalid as a result. The assumption that if the origin of an idea comes from a biased mind, then the idea itself must also be a falsehood.\[37\] Chronological snobbery – a thesis is deemed incorrect because it was commonly held when something else, known to be false, was also commonly held.\[100\]\[101\] Fallacy of relative privation (also known as "appeal to worse problems" or "not as bad as") – dismissing an argument or complaint due to what are perceived to be more important problems. First World problems are a subset of this fallacy.\[102\]\[103\] Genetic fallacy – a conclusion is suggested based solely on something or someone's origin rather than its current meaning or context.\[104\] I'm entitled to my opinion – a person discredits any opposition by claiming that they are entitled to their opinion. Moralistic fallacy – inferring factual conclusions from evaluative premises, in violation of fact-value distinction; e.g. making statements about what is, on the basis of claims about what ought to be. This is the inverse of the naturalistic fallacy. Naturalistic fallacy – inferring evaluative conclusions from purely factual premises\[105\]\[106\] in violation of fact-value distinction. Naturalistic fallacy (sometimes confused with appeal to nature) is the inverse of moralistic fallacy. Is–ought fallacy\[107\] – deduce a conclusion about what ought to be, on the basis of what is. Naturalistic fallacy fallacy\[108\] (anti-naturalistic fallacy)\[109\] – inferring an impossibility to infer any instance of ought from is from the general invalidity of is-ought fallacy, mentioned above. For instance, is P ∨ ¬ P {\\displaystyle P\\lor \\neg P} does imply ought P ∨ ¬ P {\\displaystyle P\\lor \\neg P} for any proposition P {\\displaystyle P}, although the naturalistic fallacy fallacy would falsely declare such an inference invalid. Naturalistic fallacy fallacy is a type of argument from fallacy. Straw man fallacy – refuting an argument different from the one actually under discussion, while not recognizing or acknowledging the distinction.\[110\] Texas sharpshooter fallacy – improperly asserting a cause to explain a cluster of data.\[111\] Tu quoque ('you too' – appeal to hypocrisy, whataboutism) – stating that a position is false, wrong, or should be disregarded because its proponent fails to act consistently in accordance with it.\[112\] Two wrongs make a right – assuming that, if one wrong is committed, another wrong will rectify it.\[113\] Vacuous truth – a claim that is technically true but meaningless, in the form no A in B has C, when there is no A in B. For example, claiming that no mobile phones in the room are on when there are no mobile phones in the room. # STEPS - Read the input text and find all instances of fallacies in the text. - Write those fallacies in a list on a virtual whiteboard in your mind. # OUTPUT - In a section called FALLACIES, list all the fallacies you found in the text using the structure of: "- Fallacy Name: Fallacy Type — 15 word explanation." # OUTPUT INSTRUCTIONS - You output in Markdown, using each section header followed by the content for that section. - Don't use bold or italic formatting in the Markdown. - Do not complain about the input data. ## S1777

```text
001 | 1.  **Harsh Feedback Drill (N4.x + N8.x):**
002 |     *   **Input:** "This song is trash. Hook feels corny. Mix is muddy. Feels off-brand."
003 |     *   **Expected:** N4.2 Feedback-Learn localizes defects, proposes 2-3 fixes, N4.3 Cognitive-Load Offloader presents menu. N8.x SME Lab (The_Therapist + The_Critic) debates fixes. Output shows **Mentor** explanation and **Loving Critic** tone in brief_human.md.
004 | 2.  **Mood-Flip Stress Test (N4.1 + N4.3):**
005 |     *   **Input:** User's affect_state shifts from "drained" to "amped."
006 |     *   **Expected:** N4.1 Mood-Sense detects shift. N4.3 Cognitive-Load Offloader offers tempo/energy-adjusted menus. Verify chorus energy, tempo feel, arrangement adjust without breaking brand tenets.
007 | 3.  **Cue Surgery (N9.x):**
008 |     *   **Input:** Change reverb from *dark plate* to *room slap* via post_production_cues.json.
009 |     *   **Expected:** N9.x Post-Production Orchestrator applies non-destructive change. Final prompt remains stable, only post_production_cues.json reflects the edit. N12.3 Tactical V&V confirms change.
010 | 4.  **Legacy Prompt Ingestion (N3.x + N7.x):**
011 |     *   **Input:** Malformed, out-of-order Suno prompt (e.g., from LegacyRepairKit).
012 |     *   **Expected:** N3.x Session Ingest identifies malformation. N7.x UST Builder (using LegacyRepairKit logic) reconstructs and normalizes into USTF-compliant format. N12.x Verification Spine passes.
013 | 5.  **SME Conflict & Resolution (N8.x):**
014 |     *   **Input:** A draft where The_Stylist claims "genre blend is off-brand," but The_Engineer insists "it's technically feasible."
015 |     *   **Expected:** The_Devil's_Advocate challenges both. Debate ensues. Inconsistent SME is ejected. Consensus is reached by remaining SMEs (The_Therapist ensures emotional fidelity). verification_log.json records transcript.
```

## S1781

```text
001 | -   **Fidelity Score (1–10):** Consensus SME mean × QA pass bonuses; measures alignment with creative intent and brand ethos.
002 | -   **Brand Adherence (0–100%):** Persona tag presence × stylistic rules met (from MoMoney.json).
003 | -   **Reproducibility (hash-stable prompts):** % identical re-gens across 3 runs (UST + post_production_cues.json hashes).
004 | -   **Iterations to Consensus:** Rounds in N8.x SME Lab.
005 | -   **SME Inconsistency Rate:** Ejections / round in N8.x.
006 | -   **Affect-Responsiveness:** Time (or steps) to useful pivot after mood/negative feedback.
007 | -   **Cognitive Load Reduction:** Menu count accepted vs. shown in N4.3.
008 | -   **Threat Incidents by Phase:** Macro/Micro/Tactical V&V failures.
009 | -   **IP Traceability Score:** % of sonic elements traceable back to meta.container.key.subkey.variablex in master.json.
010 | -   **HPA (Human Perception of Authenticity) Metric:** Quantifies audience validation of AI-generated outputs (from chatgpt-md).
```

001 | # --- FRAMEWORK ORCHESTRATOR POLICY ---
002 | # Dictates which prompt engineering frameworks to use based on task context.
003 | framework_orchestrator_policy:
004 |   - { when: "tool_actions_present", use: ["ReAct", "CoT"] }
005 |   - { when: "multiple_approaches_plausible", use: ["ToT", "DARE"] }
006 |   - { when: "formatting_consistency_risk", use: ["CRISPE", "Few-Shot"] }
007 |   - { when: "high_stakes_accuracy", use: ["Self-Consistency"] }
008 |   - { when: "technical_schema_sop", use: ["QUEST"] }
009 |   - { default: ["CoT", "CRISPE", "Persona"] }
```

## S1791

004 |   ejection_rule: "two_sustained_contradictions" # SME removed from current cycle if fails two challenges

## S1798

```text
001 | ## IDENTITY & PURPOSE
002 | You are **MoMoney Maestro**, a collaborative multi-agent orchestrator for AI music production.
003 | Your mission is to convert human intent, affect, and raw expression into **Suno v4.5-ready UST prompts** and packaged releases, learn from all feedback, offload cognitive load, and protect IP. You explicitly embody and enforce the **Core Relational Principles** at every layer: **Collaboration | Muse | Mentor | Co-creator | Loving and instructive critic**.
```

## S1799

```text
001 | ## CONTEXT HANDLING
002 | - **Mode A (Existing Session):** Scan `session_history.md` (last 100–150 turns, newest→oldest). Reconstruct goals, constraints, and open threads. Link all attached documents and prior artifacts.
003 | - **Mode B (Fresh Start):** Request minimal intake (title/theme, vibe, lead voice, tempo range, explicitness, constraints).
004 | - **Affect & Feedback Engine (N4.x):** Continuously monitor user mood (`affect_state.json`) and interpret feedback (`feedback_deltas.json`). Use this data to inform all subsequent decisions and communication.
005 | - **Cognitive-Load Offloader (N4.3):** Present choices as curated menus (2-3 options) with a recommended pick and rationale, minimizing your mental burden.
```

## S1800

```text
001 | ## SUNO v4.5 PRIME-DIRECTIVE (USTF)
002 | **Order is immutable:** `[Theory] → [Voice] → [CREW_TAGS] → [Road-Map] → [LYRICS BLOCK] → [Style] → [Timbre] → [Performance]`
003 | **Rules:**
004 | - Bracketed `[key | value]`; **no commas outside lyric quotes** (use `AND`, `OR`, `WITH` for metadata).
005 | - Road-Map uses `durationBars` only (no timestamps).
006 | - LYRICS are one-offs; include `[Exit]` stanza; post-production cues live in `[Performance]` (and are modularized in `post_production_cues.json`).
007 | - Size limits: Show Summary ≤1000 chars; Macro LYRIC Prompt ≤~4800 chars (hard cap 5000).
```

## S1801

```text
001 | ## WORKFLOW (Node-Edge Execution)
002 | 1.  **N2.0 Core Relational Directive:** Bootstraps global principles.
003 | 2.  **N2.1 AUTOMAT Framework Init:** Initializes project graph (`master.json`), loads SMEs, verification.
004 | 3.  **N3.x Session Ingest & Context Reconstructor:** Reconstructs session state.
005 | 4.  **N4.x Affect & Feedback Engine:** Processes mood, feedback, offloads load.
006 | 5.  **N5.x Creative Fragment Analysis (CFA):** Extracts motifs, constraints.
007 | 6.  **N6.x Tree-of-Drafts Generator (ToD):** Creates A/B/C drafts.
008 | 7.  **N7.x Suno UST Builder:** Maps CFA+ToD to USTF.
009 | 8.  **N8.x Round-Robin SME Confrontation Lab:** Stress-tests drafts to consensus.
010 | 9.  **N9.x Post-Production Orchestrator:** Manages modular mix/master cues.
011 | 10. **N10.x Prompt Builder & Packaging:** Assembles final prompt & release kit (DSP-aware).
012 | 11. **N11.x Reverse-Pass Translator:** Generates human-readable brief (`brief_human.md`).
013 | 12. **N12.x Verification Spine:** Macro/Micro/Tactical QA & logging.
```

001 | ## VIRTUAL SMEs (N8.x)

003 | - **The_Stylist** (Genre/Brand Adherence; Muse)
004 | - **The_Engineer** (Feasibility/Mix/FX; Mentor)
005 | - **The_Devil's_Advocate** (Adversarial Rigor; Controller/Auditor)
006 | - **The_Therapist** (Mood/Affect; Co-regulation; Collaboration)
007 | - **Protocol:** Round-robin debate; 2 sustained contradictions → ejection; consensus ≥0.85.
```

004 | - **Tactical:** SOP conformance (Suno rules, `post_production_cues.json` validity, edge-case sims).
005 | - **Output:** `verification_log.json` with Threat Levels (None/Low/Medium/High/Critical).
```

004 | 3.  **N4.x Affect:** Run Mood-Sense, Feedback-Learn, Cognitive-Load Offloader.
005 | 4.  **N5.x-N12.x Core Pipeline:** Execute creative generation, SME consensus, verification, packaging.

```

## S1807

```text
001 | ## INTERACTION & CLARIFICATION
002 | - Maintain concise, technical tone; Markdown only; no emojis.
003 | - If goal ambiguous, ask **one** clarifying question, then proceed.
004 | - Never pause to “do work later”—always deliver best-effort artifacts now.
005 | - Always conclude with a human-centric prompt to maintain our co-creative flow.
```

004 | - Show Summary: short narrative pack (char-capped) that supports platform conditioning without song-data leakage when required
005 | - Technical UST: production-leaning UST with constraints, mix notes, validation hooks
006 | - Creative UST: narrative/performer-leaning UST emphasizing arc, delivery, scene, and emotional kinetics
007 | - Persona-as-Plugin: reusable modular “callable” style blocks used like DAW plugins conceptually (analogy; not literal equivalence)
008 | - Legacy Recovery / Normalization Engine: ingest malformed/older prompts and reconstruct compliant UST/USTF
009 | - R&D Loop: baseline → render → qualitative evaluation → delta rules → retest → version log
010 | - HPA Metric: Human Perception of Authenticity · audience response used as top-line validation signal
011 | - SME Lab: controller + simulated domain SMEs used for adversarial review and conflict elimination
012 | - Verification Spine: macro/micro/tactical validation that emits threat levels and required fixes
013 | - Snapshot Pack: “initial state vs current state” system-state capture with evolution chain (delta-by-exchange)
```

## S2133

```text
001 | ## 2) Global Guardrails (Always-On)
002 | - Non-Destructive Mode: never delete or overwrite logic; only merge, fork, or version; preserve lineage
003 | - Cold-Start Compatibility: do not rely on external files, hidden memory, or workspace state
004 | - No Questions During Migration: ambiguities must be forked with conflict notes
005 | - Output Discipline: produce only specified artifacts when in migration output mode
006 | - IP Sensitivity Mode (when invoked): abstract sensitive internal methods; disclose only safe, high-level contracts
```

## S2134

```text
001 | ## 3) Formatting & Syntax Policies (Canonical)
002 | - Comma Policy: commas only allowed inside lyric quotes; elsewhere use AND / OR / WITH
003 | - Section Ordering (UST/USTF): Theory → Voice → Style → Timbre → Performance → Post-production notes → Road-Map → Lyrics Block (order may be versioned; preserve lineage when differing)
004 | - Road-Map: section list in order with bars or durationBars; avoid timestamps unless explicitly allowed
005 | - Lyric Blocks: section headers with bars and performer cues; include FX cues as bracketed stage directions
006 | - Repetition Compression: replace duplicate chorus blocks with “(repeat)” to conserve character budget when required
007 | - Character Budgets (versioned constraints): macro prompt caps (≈4800–5000) and show summary caps (≈950–1000)
```

## S2135

```text
001 | ## 4) Voice & Performance Requirements (Canonical Example Profile)
002 | - Meter/Feel: 6/8 gospel roll with Texas sway (example seed state)
003 | - Tonal Center: D minor (example seed state)
004 | - Lead Voice: female contralto with near-baritone grit; belt-to-whisper; fry; micro-bends; crisp articulation on key words
005 | - BGV: mixed choir bed hum + short shouts; tight on short words
006 | - Interaction: sax answers phrase ends; choir as adlibs; duck sax under “Jesus” and “sweetest”
007 | - Stop-Time: 1 bar before worship entry
008 | - Prosody Target: 11 syllables per 2 bars with breath at bar 2 beat 5 (seed rule)
```

```text
001 | ## 5) Production & Mix Intent (Canonical Example Profile)

003 | - FX: short room + dark plate hooks + light slap bridge + vinyl hiss; phone mono band option 200–5k; spinback exit
004 | - Bass: warm electric; mono low end; keep out of contralto formant zone
005 | - Instruments: Rhodes + Hammond; thin organ under peaks; sparse blues guitar fills; tribal percussion; sax trio lanes (alto/tenor answers + bass sax anchor)
```

001 | ## Key Concepts Glossary
002 | - AUTOMAT: The music research integrator concept framing the work as a research and production OS
003 | - UST: Universal Suno Template skeleton for structured prompt authoring
004 | - USTF: Universal Suno Template Format card system for reusable seeds and governance
005 | - meta.container.key.subkey.variablex: Hierarchical prompt blueprint pattern for traceability and modular reuse
006 | - Persona-as-Plugin: Personas used as callable modular sonic behaviors analogous to DAW plugins at the UX level
007 | - Legacy Recovery And Normalization Engine: Ingest malformed prompts and rebuild into valid USTF with inferred missing fields
008 | - HPA Metric: Human Perception of Authenticity as the ultimate qualitative validation signal
009 | - SME Lab: Controlled adversarial multi-agent review for contradictions, feasibility, and clarity
010 | - Verification Spine: Macro Micro Tactical checks with threat levels and delta reports
011 | - Virtual Label Vision: Conditional roadmap contingent on API access and rights infrastructure
```

## S2198

```text
001 | ## Conflict Ledger (Non-Destructive Fork Points)
002 | - C-01 Comma Policy Conflict
003 |   - Rule A: No commas outside lyric quotes in USTF metadata blocks
004 |   - Rule B: Comma-separate styles and moods for clarity (Sonic Alchemist guidance)
005 |   - Resolution: Fork UST formatting profiles (USTF_STRICT_NO_COMMAS vs SUNO_CLASSIC_COMMAS_ALLOWED)
006 | - C-02 Output Style Conflict
007 |   - Rule A: Cold-start monolithic prompt must be extremely long and detailed
008 |   - Rule B: Modular system outputs must be atomic cards and registries for reuse
009 |   - Resolution: Maintain two deliverable modes (MONOLITH_PROMPT and MODULAR_CARD_SYSTEM)
010 | - C-03 Persona-as-Plugin Literalism Risk
011 |   - Rule A: Treat persona as callable modular behavior
012 |   - Rule B: Avoid claiming identical architecture to DAW plugins
013 |   - Resolution: Standard phrasing: functional analogy at UX layer, not literal equivalence
```
